# Session transcript

Generated by `bin/transcript` from the Claude Code JSONL log. Every prompt,
reply, tool call and tool result, in order and in full.

Three things to know about what is here:

- **The model's reasoning is not in it.** Every `thinking` block in the log is
  an empty string carrying only a signature, so it is not persisted and cannot
  be recovered. What is below is what was said and done, not what was weighed.
- **Tool results and injected context are collapsed.** Expand them for the
  detail; the conversation reads straight through without. Context the harness
  injected is labelled as such rather than counted as something anybody said.
- **Only headings were altered.** Heading levels inside replies are demoted by
  two so they do not collide with the turn headings. Nothing else is changed.

# FEATURES

These are new items on the roadmap. There are more in BACKLOG.md.

## Context Management

AGENTS.md and DECISIONS.md are growing. Does it makes sense to compress them in the interest of saving tokens? Or do agents read only in segments as relevant?

## Pending Invites

Does the identifier column make sense? Shouldn't it be an account id or email?

## Add Contact

Is email sent inviting user to install app and accept invitation?

## Other Audio Output

A playing audio in some other app should be paused when audio activity, in or out, in The Floor begins.

## Immediate Play and Export

Mixing the file should happen immediately after recording completes, playback and export are immediate.

## Notify Channel Member

Ping specific member with a notification with specific text. Limited char count.

## Start a Channel

Replace button to start a channel with something simpler. No picker, start channel with only self and invite from there.

## Review Logic for States

Self-Mute, Muted-by-Claim, Claimed Floor, In-App, Present-in-Channel, Mic Open, Speaking, Recording, Playing, Audio Connected, Audio Output Selection.

For each of these and perhaps others: determine its name in the source code, determine its conditions according to the source, and further specify its conditions where these disagree.

Allow recording when alone.

Multiple members present, audio from background app with its output set to bluetooth speakers. Does audio loop back into mic and into channel?

Self-muting in a channel with other speakers should not disconnect output from bluetooth headphones.

## Redesign User Cards

In both Home and Channel views, user cards need redeign.

## Channel Admins

Channels, by default, have no admins or owner. In channel settings, a user can declare himself owner, and then give admin status to others. Certain functions are now available only to admins, and owner who is an admin implicitly.

## Track Usage

Per user tracking of minutes and timestamps of webrtc usage, minutes and timestamps of media playback including recordings playback, minutes and timestamps of recordings associated to user who initiates the recording, minutes of transcripts requested, GBs of recording egress/exports.

Some of this will stay in db. Some will be visible and exportable to users.

## Media Playback quality

Suppose two users are in a channel, both are muted and they are playing media. Is the quality of the playback equivalent to playing it directly or is it diminished by passing over webrtc? I sometimes get the impression that quality varies even during the playbook of a single file. Volume also seems to rise and fall, without manual intervention. Maybe this is a feature of webrtc?

## Transcripts

Implement integration with Assembly.ai. Use multi-channel transcripts, searchability, batch transcription (not streaming), multi-language, diarization or speak-identification. Transcript triggered manually on recordings, result attached to recording and exportable. Search available during playback, and also across set of recording in channel.

## Watch Party

Currently, media play allows uploaded audio to be played and included into exportable recordings. Independently of this functionality, a watch party plays video, and disallows recordings. Plan is here: WATCHPARTY.md

## Availability Logic

By way of indicators and notifications, users know when their contacts are available for conversartion, without having to interrupt each other with disruptive phone calls.

## Phone Calls

What happens when user receives a phone call?

## Anonymous Web Access

  account_id TEXT NOT NULL REFERENCES accounts(id),
  created_at INTEGER NOT NULL,
  expires_at INTEGER NOT NULL

CREATE INDEX IF NOT EXISTS tokens_account ON tokens(account_id);

-- A contact pair is stored once, with a_id < b_id so the pair has a single
-- canonical row regardless of who asked. requester_id records the direction,
-- which is what distinguishes an incoming request from an outgoing one.
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS contacts (
  a_id         TEXT NOT NULL REFERENCES accounts(id),
  b_id         TEXT NOT NULL REFERENCES accounts(id),
  state        TEXT NOT NULL CHECK (state IN ('pending', 'accepted')),
  requester_id TEXT NOT NULL,
  created_at   INTEGER NOT NULL,
  PRIMARY KEY (a_id, b_id)
);

-- A contact request sent to an address with no account yet.
--
-- Requests must be storable whether or not the recipient exists, or the
-- interface answers a question it should not: a real request produces a
-- pending row and an imaginary one produces nothing, which tells anyone who
-- looks whether an address has an account here. Keeping both means every
-- request looks the same — and it lets someone invite a friend before that
-- friend has signed up, which is worth having on its own.
--
-- Resolved into a real contacts row the first time that address signs in.
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS pending_invites (
  requester_id TEXT NOT NULL REFERENCES accounts(id),
  identifier   TEXT NOT NULL,
  created_at   INTEGER NOT NULL,
  PRIMARY KEY (requester_id, identifier)
);

-- Money somebody gave, voluntarily, toward keeping this running. Nothing is
-- unlocked by it: an account that has never given a penny behaves identically
-- to one that has, which is what keeps this table off every read path in the
-- application.
--
-- Keyed on Ko-fi's transaction id because that is the whole idempotency story.
-- A webhook is retried on any answer they do not like, and INSERT OR IGNORE
-- against this key makes a replay a no-op without a read first.
--
-- account_id is nullable on purpose. Ko-fi's donate link carries no passthrough
-- field, so the account is matched afterwards rather than known at the time,
-- and a donation from somebody with no account here is still a donation. Losing
-- it because it could not be attributed would be the wrong trade in every
-- direction.
--
-- Matching is by address alone, and a donation paid from an address nobody has
-- signed in with is left unattributed rather than guessed at. An earlier
-- version inferred the giver from whoever had most recently tapped Support;
-- that credits the wrong person whenever two people are donating at once, and
-- nothing afterwards would ever reveal it had. An unattributed row is visible
-- and fixable; a confidently wrong one is neither.
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS donations (
  kofi_transaction_id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
  account_id   TEXT REFERENCES accounts(id),
  -- 'email' when the payer's address is one we know, 'manual' when somebody
  -- resolved it by hand from Ko-fi's dashboard or a CSV export.
  matched_by   TEXT CHECK (matched_by IN ('email', 'manual')),
  email        TEXT,
  from_name    TEXT,
  message      TEXT,
  -- Integer cents. Ko-fi sends "3.00" as a string; it is parsed straight to
  -- cents rather than through a float, because money in a float is a defect
  -- waiting for a large enough number.
  amount_cents INTEGER NOT NULL,
  currency     TEXT NOT NULL,
  -- Ko-fi's own type field: Donation, Subscription, Shop Order, Commission.
```

  // --- Contacts -----------------------------------------------------------

  /**
   * Turns requests sent to this address before it had an account into real
   * pending contact requests, now that it does.
   *
   * Someone who signs up therefore finds whoever invited them already waiting,
   * which is the point of storing the request in the first place.
   */
  private resolveInvitesFor(account: AccountRow): void {
    const invites = this.db
      .prepare(
        'SELECT requester_id FROM pending_invites WHERE identifier = ? COLLATE NOCASE'
      )
      .all(account.identifier) as Array<{ requester_id: string }>;

    for (const { requester_id } of invites) {
      if (requester_id === account.id) continue;
      const [a, b] = pairKey(requester_id, account.id);
      this.db
        .prepare(
          `INSERT OR IGNORE INTO contacts (a_id, b_id, state, requester_id, created_at)
           VALUES (?, ?, 'pending', ?, ?)`
        )
        .run(a, b, requester_id, account.created_at);
    }

    this.db
      .prepare('DELETE FROM pending_invites WHERE identifier = ? COLLATE NOCASE')
      .run(account.identifier);
  }

  /**
   * The contact list, including requests sent to addresses with no account.
   *
   * An outgoing request is shown as the address it was sent to rather than the
===500
    return this.contactState(x, y)?.state === 'accepted';
  }

  /** Adding is never one-directional; the pair is mutual only once accepted. */
  requestContact(
    from: string,
    identifier: string,
    now: number
  ):
    // targetId is null when the address has no account yet: there is nobody to
    // notify, which the caller has to know without the answer reaching a user.
    | { ok: true; accepted: boolean; targetId: string | null }
    | { ok: false; error: string } {
    const id = normalize(identifier);
    const target = this.byIdentifier(id);

    // No account with that address: the request is stored against the address
    // itself and resolves if they ever sign up. Deliberately indistinguishable
    // from a request to a real account — refusing here would answer whether an
    // address has an account, one guess at a time.
    if (!target) {
      const me = this.byId(from);
      if (me && me.identifier.toLowerCase() === id.toLowerCase()) {
        return { ok: false, error: 'That’s you.' };
      }
      const already = this.db
        .prepare(
          'SELECT 1 FROM pending_invites WHERE requester_id = ? AND identifier = ? COLLATE NOCASE'
        )
        .get(from, id);
      if (already) return { ok: false, error: 'Request already sent.' };

      this.db
        .prepare(
          'INSERT INTO pending_invites (requester_id, identifier, created_at) VALUES (?, ?, ?)'
        )
        .run(from, id, now);
      return { ok: true, accepted: false, targetId: null };
    }

    return this.requestKnown(from, target.id, now);
  }

  /**
   * Asks somebody to be a contact when you already know who they are.
   *
===695
   * address, no name, no bio, nothing anybody can sign in as and nothing that
   * describes a human being. `public()` gives it the same shape as any other
   * account, so a stale id in an old participant list resolves to something
   * rather than to nothing, and every screen already falls back gracefully when
   * it does not.
   *
   * The identifier is replaced rather than nulled — the column is NOT NULL
   * UNIQUE, and a value derived from the id is unique by construction. It is
   * deliberately not an email address, so the same person signing up again gets
   * a genuinely new account rather than walking back into this one.
   *
   * What goes with it:
   *
   * - **Contacts**, both directions and both states. Nobody keeps a relationship
   *   with an account that is gone, and nobody is left holding the address.
   * - **Invitations**, sent and received: rows this account sent to addresses
   *   that never signed up, and rows other people sent to *its* address, which
   *   would otherwise resolve into a contact request from a stranger if that
   *   address ever signed up again.
   * - **Sign-in codes and tokens**, so every device is signed out at once,
   *   including any this person no longer has.
   *
   * Donations are **unlinked rather than deleted**: they are money that changed
   * hands, Ko-fi holds the authoritative record either way, and a payment
   * disappearing from this side because the payer left would leave the two ends
   * disagreeing with nothing to reconcile from. `matched_by` goes with the link
   * it describes, so a row cannot claim to be matched to nobody.
   *
   * Devices are `Devices.forgetAccount`'s job and are not reached from here —
   * this class owns identity, that one owns addresses, and the route calls both.
   */
  erase(accountId: string): boolean {
    const account = this.byId(accountId);
    if (!account) return false;

    this.db
      .prepare('DELETE FROM contacts WHERE a_id = ? OR b_id = ?')
      .run(accountId, accountId);
    this.db
      .prepare('DELETE FROM pending_invites WHERE requester_id = ?')
      .run(accountId);
    this.db
      .prepare('DELETE FROM pending_invites WHERE identifier = ? COLLATE NOCASE')
      .run(account.identifier);
    this.db
      .prepare('DELETE FROM otp_codes WHERE identifier = ? COLLATE NOCASE')
      .run(account.identifier);
    this.db.prepare('DELETE FROM tokens WHERE account_id = ?').run(accountId);
    this.db
      .prepare(
        'UPDATE donations SET account_id = NULL, matched_by = NULL WHERE account_id = ?'
      )
      .run(accountId);

    this.db
      .prepare(
        `UPDATE accounts
            SET identifier = ?, display_name = ?, bio = NULL,
                last_seen_at = NULL, donations_allowed = NULL
          WHERE id = ?`
      )
      .run(erasedIdentifier(accountId), ERASED_DISPLAY_NAME, accountId);
    return true;
  }
}

  // --- Contacts -----------------------------------------------------------

  fastify.post('/contacts/request', async (request, reply) => {
    const account = await requireAccount(request, reply);
    if (!account) return;

    const body = request.body as { identifier?: string } | undefined;
    if (!body?.identifier) {
      return reply.code(400).send({ error: 'identifier is required' });
    }

    const result = accounts.requestContact(account.id, body.identifier, now());
    if (!result.ok) return reply.code(400).send({ error: result.error });
    // The recipient is the whole point: without telling them, a request simply
    // never appears on their side.
    // No target to notify when the address has no account yet.
    homeNotifier.notify(
      result.targetId ? [account.id, result.targetId] : [account.id]
    );
    return { ok: true, accepted: result.accepted };
  });

  // Withdrawal goes by address, not row id — see Accounts.withdrawRequest for
  // why an id would give away what the empty outgoing id exists to hide.
  fastify.post('/contacts/withdraw', async (request, reply) => {
    const account = await requireAccount(request, reply);
    if (!account) return;

    const body = request.body as { identifier?: string } | undefined;

      return reply.code(400).send({ error: 'identifier is required' });
    }

    const result = accounts.withdrawRequest(account.id, body.identifier);
    if (!result.withdrawn) {
      return reply.code(400).send({ error: 'No pending request to that address.' });
    }
    // The recipient, if there is one, has a request vanishing from their Home.
    homeNotifier.notify(
      result.targetId ? [account.id, result.targetId] : [account.id]

Answered from the code rather than from the roadmap note. Here's what I found.

**The column is right, and it can't be either of the alternatives.**

`pending_invites` (`server/src/db.ts:158`) exists precisely for the case where *no account exists*, so an account id is impossible by construction — that's the table's whole reason for being, and the comment above it says so: storing the request whether or not the recipient exists is what stops the interface leaking which addresses have accounts here. If the column were an account id, a request to a stranger would have nothing to store, and the difference between "produced a row" and "produced nothing" would be a membership oracle you could query one guess at a time.

And it already *is* the email. `identifier` is this codebase's name for the sign-in address throughout — `accounts.identifier`, `otp_codes.identifier`, the `/auth/request-code` body, the `/contacts/request` body. Renaming this one column to `email` would make it the only table that calls the same value something different, and the resolution join at `server/src/accounts.ts:402` matches `pending_invites.identifier` against `accounts.identifier` directly, so the two names have to stay recognizably the same thing. The abstraction also isn't idle: if a phone number ever becomes a sign-in address, `identifier` accommodates it and `email` would have to be migrated.

**One real gap, found while checking this.** `/auth/request-code` validates with `isEmailAddress` (`server/src/app.ts:286`); `/contacts/request` does not (`server/src/app.ts:392-409`). It passes the raw string to `requestContact`, which — finding no account — writes it to `pending_invites` verbatim. So `bob`, `  `, or a 10KB string all become permanent invite rows that can never resolve, because signing up requires an email and nothing else ever matches them. Nothing sweeps the table; the only deletions are on successful resolution and on account erase. The client doesn't guard it either — `HomeView.tsx:684` is a plain text input labelled "Search by email address".

The fix is to apply the same `isEmailAddress` check in `/contacts/request` (and `/contacts/withdraw`, so a withdrawal can still reach a row written before the check). It has to stay a *format* check only — rejecting a well-formed address that has no account would re-open the enumeration hole the table was designed to close.

I stopped at the diagnosis rather than editing, since the roadmap item asked a design question and the validation change alters what the API accepts. Say the word and I'll make it in a worktree.

result:
`pending_invites.identifier` is correct as-is — an account id is impossible (the table's purpose is invitees with no account) and it already holds the email under the codebase-wide name; but `/contacts/request` skips the `isEmailAddress` check that `/auth/request-code` applies, so malformed addresses become permanently unresolvable rows.

> _system:_ Answered the FEATURES.md "Pending Invites" question: the identifier column is correct as-is, since invitees have no account yet. I also found that /contacts/request skips email validation. Next: say the word and I'll add that check in a worktree. (disable recaps in /config)

Withdraw must stay permissive, or the junk rows already in the table become unremovable. Validation goes on `/contacts/request` only.

export interface Mailer {
  /** Resolves once handed to the transport. Delivery itself is not guaranteed. */
  sendCode(to: string, code: string): Promise<void>;
}

export function isEmailAddress(identifier: string): boolean {
  // Deliberately loose. SES is the real validator; this only decides which
  // transport an identifier belongs to.
  return /^[^\s@]+@[^\s@]+\.[^\s@]+$/.test(identifier.trim());
}

export interface SesMailerOptions {
  from: string;
  region: string;
  /** Overrides the default credential chain. Omit to use the environment. */
  clientConfig?: SESv2ClientConfig;
}

The tests reveal something that changes the fix: contact requests are made to **phone numbers** (`+15550000002`) throughout `ws.test.ts` and `server.test.ts`. That's deliberate — `app.ts:281` notes sign-in is email-only *for now* but the spec allows a phone number. An email-only check on `/contacts/request` would break the designed-for identifier. Let me check what predicates exist.

**→ Bash**

```json
{
  "command": "grep -rn \"phone\\|E.164\\|^\\+1\\|isPhone\" server/src/*.ts core/*.ts 2>/dev/null | grep -vi \"telephone\" | head -20",
  "description": "Look for a phone predicate"
}
```

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/Users/rodvandur/src/thefloor/.claude/worktrees/invite-identifier-validation/server/src/mail.ts",
  "old_string": "export function isEmailAddress(identifier: string): boolean {\n  // Deliberately loose. SES is the real validator; this only decides which\n  // transport an identifier belongs to.\n  return /^[^\\s@]+@[^\\s@]+\\.[^\\s@]+$/.test(identifier.trim());\n}",
  "new_string": "export function isEmailAddress(identifier: string): boolean {\n  // Deliberately loose. SES is the real validator; this only decides which\n  // transport an identifier belongs to.\n  return /^[^\\s@]+@[^\\s@]+\\.[^\\s@]+$/.test(identifier.trim());\n}\n\n/**\n * The longest identifier worth storing. 254 is the maximum length of an email\n * address that can actually be delivered to, and no phone number approaches it.\n */\nexport const MAX_IDENTIFIER_LENGTH = 254;\n\n/** Loose on purpose, the way isEmailAddress is: shape rather than reachability. */\nexport function isPhoneNumber(identifier: string): boolean {\n  return /^\\+?[0-9][0-9\\s().-]{6,19}$/.test(identifier.trim());\n}\n\n/**\n * Whether an identifier could name somebody at all, by either transport.\n *\n * Wider than `isEmailAddress`, and the width is the point. Sign-in is email\n * only today, but a phone number is the second identifier the design reserves\n * and contact requests are already made to one — so the test for \"is this an\n * address\" and the test for \"can we mail a code to this\" are not the same\n * question, and answering the first with the second would decide against SMS\n * from the one place with no stake in it.\n *\n * What this exists to exclude is the identifier that can never resolve into\n * anybody: a bare word, a sentence, a kilobyte of text. Those become permanent\n * rows in pending_invites, which nothing sweeps.\n */\nexport function isPlausibleIdentifier(identifier: string): boolean {\n  const id = identifier.trim();\n  if (id.length === 0 || id.length > MAX_IDENTIFIER_LENGTH) return false;\n  return isEmailAddress(id) || isPhoneNumber(id);\n}"
}

  "new_string": "    // A request to an address with no account is stored verbatim in\n    // pending_invites and resolves if that address ever signs up. Nothing\n    // sweeps that table — its only deletions are on resolution and on erase —\n    // so an identifier that could never name anybody is a row that is\n    // permanent and unreachable at once. This is the only place that check can\n    // happen, since by then the address is a primary key.\n    //\n    // Shape only, and deliberately wider than sign-in's email test: see\n    // isPlausibleIdentifier. Refusing a well-formed address because no account\n    // holds it would answer, one guess at a time, exactly the question\n    // pending_invites exists to leave unanswered — see db.ts.\n    if (!isPlausibleIdentifier(body.identifier)) {\n      return reply\n        .code(400)\n        .send({ error: 'Enter an email address or a phone number.' });\n    }"

```
## Connecting to somebody you met in a channel

Built 2026-08-10. Tap a person in a channel's roster to see their profile,
and ask them to be a contact from there.

The obstacle was never the interface. Contact requests went by email address,
and meeting somebody in a channel an acquaintance opened gives you their name
and their account id and nothing else — so the existing path would have meant
showing you their address first, which is theirs to give out rather than ours
to disclose so that a button can work. Hence a by-id request route, sharing its
whole second half with the by-address one: the difference between them is only
how the other person is named.

**Sharing a channel is what entitles you to ask.** Account ids travel in every
roster, so without that check an id would be a way to pester anyone who ever
appeared in one. Refusals answer 404, identical to a nonexistent id, so the
route cannot be used to find out which ids are real — the same rule, and the
same reasoning, as reading a profile.

**Being in a channel together is permission to ask, not consent to be
anybody's contact.** It sends an ordinary pending request and the other person
decides, exactly as an emailed one does. Somebody who has already asked you
gets the existing treatment: the request goes straight through as an
acceptance, on the grounds that asking somebody who asked you is agreement.

---

## Presence is exclusive

Fixed 2026-08-10, after being reachable in the shipped app: go Home from a
channel, tap a second one, and you were left marked present in both.
```

</details>

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/Users/rodvandur/src/thefloor/.claude/worktrees/invite-identifier-validation/planning/DECISIONS.md",
  "old_string": "---\n\n## Presence is exclusive",
  "new_string": "---\n\n## `pending_invites.identifier` stays an identifier, and is now checked\n\nSettled 2026-08-15, from a roadmap question asking whether the column should\nhold an account id or an email instead.\n\n**An account id is impossible by construction.** The table exists for exactly\nthe case where no account exists. Storing the request either way is what stops\nthe interface answering whether an address is registered here — a real request\nproducing a row and an imaginary one producing nothing is a membership oracle\nanyone can query one guess at a time. The comment above the table in `db.ts`\ncarries the full reasoning and is the thing to read before changing any of it.\n\n**It is already the email**, under the name this codebase gives a sign-in\naddress everywhere else: `accounts.identifier`, `otp_codes.identifier`, and the\nbodies of `/auth/request-code` and `/contacts/request`. Renaming it to `email`\nwould make one table disagree with the rest, break the resolution join in\n`resolveInvitesFor` against `accounts.identifier`, and decide against a phone\nnumber ever being an identifier — which the design still reserves.\n\nWhat the question did surface is a real gap. `/auth/request-code` validates\nwith `isEmailAddress`; `/contacts/request` validated nothing, and passed the\nraw string to `requestContact`, which wrote it to `pending_invites` verbatim\nwhenever no account matched. So `bob`, a sentence, or ten kilobytes of text\nbecame a row that can never resolve — signing up requires an address — and that\nnothing removes: the only deletions are on resolution and on erase, and there\nis no sweep.\n\nThe check added is `isPlausibleIdentifier`, and it is **deliberately wider than\nsign-in's**. Email-only was written first and broke twenty-two tests, which was\nthe useful part: contact requests are made to phone numbers throughout the\nsuite. \"Is this an address\" and \"can a code be mailed to this\" are different\nquestions, and answering the first with the second would settle the SMS\nquestion from the one place with no stake in it. So it accepts an email or a\nphone shape, caps length at 254, and rejects nothing else.\n\n**Shape only, never existence.** Refusing a well-formed address because no\naccount holds it would reopen the enumeration hole the table was built to\nclose. The two checks must not be allowed to merge.\n\n**`/contacts/withdraw` is left unvalidated, on purpose.** Rows written before\nthis hold identifiers that would not pass, and withdrawal is the only thing\nthat can remove one — validating there would make exactly those rows permanent,\nwhich is the problem rather than the fix. Nothing is exposed by the asymmetry:\nwithdrawal only ever deletes a row whose `requester_id` is already the caller's.\n\n---\n\n## Presence is exclusive"
}
```

Done. Sanity check before reporting: full suite green across all three packages (core 154, app 198, server 384), `npm run typecheck` clean, and I verified the new tests fail against the unfixed route and pass with it.

**What changed.** Three files, on branch `worktree-invite-identifier-validation`, pushed.

`server/src/mail.ts` gains `isPlausibleIdentifier` (plus `isPhoneNumber` and a 254-char cap). `server/src/app.ts` applies it in `/contacts/request`. `server/__tests__/server.test.ts` gains three cases.

**The fix is wider than what I proposed, and the tests are why.** I wrote the email-only check first and it broke 22 tests: contact requests are made to phone numbers (`+15550000002`) throughout `ws.test.ts` and `server.test.ts`, and `app.ts:281` records that a phone number is the identifier the design reserves next. "Can a code be mailed here" and "could this name a person" are different questions — settling the second with the first would have decided against SMS from the one route with no stake in it. So the check accepts an email *or* a phone shape and rejects the rest: bare words, whitespace, sentences, kilobytes of text. Shape only, never existence — refusing a well-formed address because no account holds it would reopen the enumeration hole `pending_invites` was built to close.

Those 22 failures also caught a second thing worth knowing: the worktree branched from `origin/master`, which was 14 commits behind your local `master`. I rebased onto local `master` (1b3ba13, build 36) before trusting any result.

`/contacts/withdraw` is deliberately left unvalidated, with a comment saying so — rows already written hold identifiers that wouldn't pass, and withdrawal is the only thing that can remove one.

**Two notes.** This is server-only and safe to deploy ahead of any client: installed builds already surface the `error` string from a 400 the same way they show "Request already sent.", so no wire step is needed. And I left `planning/FEATURES.md` untouched — your "Pending Invites" item is uncommitted in your working copy, so editing the worktree's older copy would only have made a conflict. The reasoning went into `planning/DECISIONS.md` as a new section; strike the roadmap item when you merge.

result:
Fixed on branch `worktree-invite-identifier-validation` (pushed, commit bb8bdbe): `/contacts/request` now rejects identifiers that could never name anybody, using a check deliberately wider than sign-in's so phone-number invites keep working — 736 tests and typecheck green, reasoning recorded in planning/DECISIONS.md.

> _system:_ Fixed the Pending Invites roadmap item: /contacts/request now rejects identifiers that can never name anyone, pushed on branch worktree-invite-identifier-validation with all tests green. Next: merge it and strike that item from FEATURES.md. (disable recaps in /config)

```
# Decisions

What was built, why it was built that way, and what it cost to find out. Also
what was considered and deliberately not built, which is the half most likely to
be mistaken for an oversight.

This is history rather than work. Nothing here is outstanding; see BACKLOG.md
for that. It is kept because the reasoning is the expensive part and it does not
survive anywhere else — a commit message is read once, by whoever is already
looking at the diff, and never again by the person about to make the same
mistake.

**This is the live volume. New decisions are appended here.** Earlier ones are
in dated volumes, which are closed and are never edited again:

| Volume | Covers | Ends at |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `DECISIONS-2026-08-07-to-2026-08-13.md` | the first decisions through self-hosting the media | the media server moving off LiveKit Cloud |
| `DECISIONS.md` — this file | 2026-08-13 onward | live |

**Keep every volume under 2,000 lines.** A plain read stops there and says so,
but the notice is easy to miss in a file that reads like an archive, and what
gets dropped is the tail — the newest and most likely to matter. When this file
approaches it, close it: rename it `DECISIONS-<first date>-to-<last date>.md`,
give it the closed-volume header the others carry, start a fresh `DECISIONS.md`
with this preamble, and add a row above. Cut on a section boundary and on a seam
that means something — an epoch in the project, not a line number.

Two sections here are exceptions to the chronology and stay in the live volume
however old they get, because they are single running records rather than dated
entries: `## The deploy history`, which is newest-first and grows at the top,
and `## The Android adaptive icon`, which describes something still unshipped.

**On vocabulary.** What this project used to call a session is now a channel,
renamed on 2026-08-10 when it stopped being a short-lived conversation and became
a permanent place. Historical passages below still name types and files as they
were at the time — `SessionView`, `SessionState` — and those are now
`ChannelView` and `ChannelState`. Two other things in this codebase are also
called sessions and are unrelated: the auth session behind a bearer token, and
LiveKit's `AudioSession`. Neither was renamed.

**And a channel is never called a room.** The word belongs to Clubhouse, and a
product that borrows a competitor's vocabulary invites the comparison it should
be avoiding. The media layer does use it — `closeRoom`, `setSilenced({ room })`,
`issueToken({ room, identity })`, `new Room(...)` in the app — because it is
LiveKit's own term for a LiveKit thing, and none of it reaches a screen. The
test is whether a user could ever read the word: in the code it is the media
plane's vocabulary; in the interface it does not exist.

---

## A card per person in a channel, lit by who is actually audible

```
#!/bin/bash
#
# Ships the server to Lightsail and restarts it.
#
# Deliberately rsync rather than git: there is no remote yet, and this keeps
# deployment to one command that works from a dirty tree. Excludes are the
# point — node_modules is rebuilt on the far side for that architecture, and
# .env there holds production secrets this must never overwrite.
#
# Live channels live in memory, so a restart drops every call in progress.
# There is no way around that yet; see planning/BACKLOG.md.
set -euo pipefail

HOST=${THEFLOOR_HOST:-ubuntu@44.241.121.49}
KEY=${THEFLOOR_KEY:-~/.ssh/lightsail-ubuntu}
SSH="ssh -i $KEY -o BatchMode=yes"

cd "$(dirname "$0")/.."

echo "→ tests"
npm test --prefix core --silent >/dev/null
npm test --prefix server --silent >/dev/null

# The .env pattern is a wildcard with one exception, matching .gitignore and for
# the same reason: a .env~ left by an editor or a .env.bak left by a careful
# person is exactly as secret-bearing as .env itself, and an exact-match exclude
# catches neither — it would sync one up, or delete one that only exists on the
# box. .env.example is the one that is documentation rather than a secret.
echo "→ syncing"
rsync -az --delete -e "$SSH" \
  --exclude node_modules --exclude .git --exclude 'app/' \
  --include '.env.example' --exclude '.env*' \
  --exclude '*.db' --exclude '*.db-*' --exclude 'dev-*.mjs' \
  --exclude '*.p8' \
  ./ "$HOST:/home/ubuntu/thefloor/"

echo "→ installing and restarting"
$SSH "$HOST" '
  cd ~/thefloor/server && npm install --omit=dev --no-audit --no-fund --silent
  sudo systemctl restart thefloor
'

echo "→ waiting for health"
for _ in $(seq 1 20); do
  if curl -sf --max-time 5 https://thefloor.rvanegas.co/healthz >/dev/null; then
```

Grounded now, and two facts decide this more than any convention does.

**`bin/deploy` rsyncs the working tree, not a git ref** — "works from a dirty tree" is deliberate. So the branch you happen to be standing on, plus any uncommitted edit, *is* what reaches production. Right now nothing records what went out: `/healthz` returns `{ok, audio}` and no version. And `bin/release-ios` bumps `buildNumber` in `app.json` without committing or tagging it, so the commit that became build 36 isn't marked anywhere.

**The deeper point: the App Store isn't a version, it's a population.** Your own docs track this constantly — "build 30 kept working across the restart", "installed builds up to 35 call `GET /support` expecting JSON and now receive HTML". The server must stay compatible with the *oldest build still in use*, which is not the newest released one and is not a thing any branch can represent.

#### What I'd recommend

**1. `master` stays trunk, and is the only thing you deploy from.** One added rule: deploy from a clean tree on `master`. That's the invariant that makes "what's on the box" knowable at all, given rsync.

**2. Tag builds; don't branch them.** A submitted build is immutable and a point in history — that's a tag, not a branch. `build/36`, created by `bin/release-ios` in the same breath as the bump it already does, so it can't be forgotten. This is your "code released to the public," and it's queryable: `git diff build/36..master` is exactly what testers have that App Store users don't.

**3. One branch, `released`, for what's downloadable right now.** It moves when a build is *released*, not when it's approved — which matches your choice of manual release, keeping approval and release two decisions. It earns its place for one reason: if review rejects build 36, you fix against what you submitted, not against a trunk that's moved on. `git diff released..master` is user-visible drift.

**4. Short-lived feature branches merged to `master`** — which is what you're already doing with `docs-split-decisions` and the worktree branches. No develop branch, no release branches. Solo, with a one-minute server deploy, git-flow would be pure ceremony.

**5. The piece branches can't express: a compatibility floor.** Record the oldest build the server still answers — a `MIN_SUPPORTED_BUILD` constant in the server, not a doc line, so it's greppable from the code that depends on it. The rule that makes it useful: a compatibility shim may be deleted only once the floor has passed the build that needed it. That's the discipline your two-step wire migration already describes; the floor is what tells you when step three is safe.

#### Two gaps worth closing at the same time

`/healthz` should report the deployed commit sha, stamped by `bin/deploy` at sync time. With rsync-from-a-dirty-tree, it is currently not possible to answer "what is actually running" from anywhere — and every "verified against production afterwards" note in your docs would be stronger anchored to a sha.

And a caution from this session: your worktree base ref is `fresh`, so `EnterWorktree` branched from `origin/master`, 14 commits behind your local `master`. That produced 22 test failures I nearly attributed to my own change. With branches mattering more, set `worktree.baseRef` to `head` — or push `master` regularly enough that `fresh` means something.

I'd note the whole convention in `AGENTS.md` (it's a trap-avoidance rule, not history) with the reasoning in `DECISIONS.md`. Want me to implement it — the tag and commit in `release-ios`, the sha stamp through `healthz`, the `MIN_SUPPORTED_BUILD` constant, and `released` created at `build/36`? Worth deciding first whether build 36 counts as `released` yet; it's `WAITING_FOR_REVIEW`, so strictly nothing is downloadable and `released` shouldn't exist until it is.

> _system:_ I answered the FEATURES question on pending invites, fixed and merged the identifier validation into docs-split-decisions, and then recommended a branching convention for App Store releases. Next: tell me whether to implement it, starting with tagging builds. (disable recaps in /config)

```
async function request<T>(
  path: string,
  options: { method?: string; body?: unknown; token?: string } = {}
): Promise<T> {
  if (!API_URL) throw new ApiError('No server configured.', 0);

  let response: Response;
  try {
    response = await fetch(API_URL + path, {
      method: options.method ?? 'GET',
      headers: {
        // Only claim a JSON body when there is one. Declaring the content type
        // on a bodyless POST makes Fastify reject it outright
        // (FST_ERR_CTP_EMPTY_JSON_BODY), which silently broke every request
        // without a payload: accept, decline, sign out, and the audio token.
        ...(options.body === undefined
          ? {}
          : { 'content-type': 'application/json' }),
        ...(options.token ? { authorization: `Bearer ${options.token}` } : {}),
      },
      body: options.body === undefined ? undefined : JSON.stringify(options.body),
    });
  } catch {
    // A refused connection here almost always means the wrong LAN address or a
    // server that is not running, so say that rather than "Network request
    // failed", which sends people looking in the wrong place.
    throw new ApiError(`Cannot reach the server at ${API_URL}.`, 0);
  }

  if (response.status === 204) return undefined as T;

```json
{
  "file_path": "/Users/rodvandur/src/thefloor/.claude/worktrees/release-branching/server/src/release.ts",
  "content": "import { readFileSync } from 'node:fs';\n\n/**\n * What this server is, and what it has promised to keep talking to.\n *\n * Two facts that look unrelated and are the same question from opposite ends:\n * which code is running here, and which clients that code owes an answer to.\n */\n\n/**\n * The oldest iOS build this server still answers correctly.\n *\n * It exists to make one decision decidable. The wire is changed in two steps —\n * teach the server the old shape as well as the new, deploy that, ship the\n * client, and remove the old shape a release later — and the third step has\n * never had a rule saying when it is safe. This is the rule: **a compatibility\n * shim may be deleted once this floor has passed the build that needed it, and\n * not before.** Anything else is a guess about whose phone still has what.\n *\n * A **declaration rather than an enforcement**, and the difference matters.\n * Nothing on the wire carries a build number — the app sends no version header\n * and the server records none — so this cannot be checked against reality, and\n * every claim in the deploy history that build N went on working was reasoned\n * rather than measured. Making it measurable is in BACKLOG.md and is the thing\n * that would give this number a source other than judgement.\n *\n * Raising it is the release decision that costs something. Build numbers rise\n * on upload; this rises only when the builds below it are gone from every\n * phone that matters, which after a public release means waiting rather than\n * deciding.\n *\n * 36 because that is the first submitted build and nothing has ever been\n * public. Every earlier build is a TestFlight install on the author's own\n * devices, updatable on demand, and TestFlight expires them in 90 days anyway.\n * The moment 36 is released this number stops being free to move.\n */\nexport const MIN_SUPPORTED_BUILD = 36;\n\nexport interface Deployed {\n  /** Short sha of the commit that was synced, `-dirty` if the tree was not clean. */\n  commit: string;\n  /** Branch it was synced from, for when the sha alone is not recognisable. */\n  branch: string;\n  /** When bin/deploy stamped it, ISO 8601. */\n  at: string;\n}\n\n/**\n * What `bin/deploy` recorded about the sync that put this code here.\n *\n * `bin/deploy` rsyncs the working tree rather than a git ref, deliberately, so\n * that it works from a dirty tree — which means nothing about what is running\n * is recoverable from the box itself. Every \"verified against production\n * afterwards\" note ever written names a behaviour and no revision. This is the\n * missing half.\n *\n * Null when the file is absent, which is every local run and is not an error:\n * a checkout is its own answer to the question.\n */\nexport function deployed(): Deployed | null {\n  // Resolved against the working directory, which the systemd unit and the npm\n  // scripts alike set to this package — the same convention as .env and the\n  // default DB_PATH.\n  try {\n    return JSON.parse(readFileSync('./deployed.json', 'utf8')) as Deployed;\n  } catch {\n    return null;\n  }\n}\n"
}

```json
{
  "replace_all": false,

  "new_string": "# What is about to be running, recorded before it goes rather than inferred\n# afterwards. This syncs like any other file and is read by server/src/release.ts.\n#\n# The dirty marker is the point. This script ships the working tree on purpose,\n# so \"which commit is on the box\" has an honest answer only when the tree was\n# clean — and the times it was not are exactly the times somebody will later\n# want to know. It warns and continues: refusing here would take away the\n# property the rsync was chosen for.\nSHA=$(git rev-parse --short HEAD)\nBRANCH=$(git rev-parse --abbrev-ref HEAD)\nif [ -n \"$(git status --porcelain)\" ]; then\n  SHA=\"$SHA-dirty\"\n  echo \"⚠ deploying a dirty tree — the box will record $SHA\" >&2\nfi\nif [ \"$BRANCH\" != \"master\" ]; then\n  echo \"⚠ deploying from $BRANCH, not master\" >&2\nfi\ncat > server/deployed.json <<JSON\n{\n  \"commit\": \"$SHA\",\n  \"branch\": \"$BRANCH\",\n  \"at\": \"$(date -u +%Y-%m-%dT%H:%M:%SZ)\"\n}\nJSON\n\necho \"→ syncing\""
}
```

There is no develop branch and no release branches.\n- **`bin/deploy` rsyncs the working tree, not a git ref** — deliberately, so it\n  works from a dirty tree. **→ Write**

```json
{
  "file_path": "/Users/rodvandur/.claude/jobs/34f700a7/tmp/branching-section.md",
  "content": "## Branches, once there was a build to be wrong about\n\nAdopted 2026-08-15, the day after build 36 was submitted. Until then there was\none branch and it did not matter: every install was the author's, and the\n2026-08-10 wire break — which stopped every running client dead — was accepted\nprecisely because of that. A submitted build ends the exemption.\n\n**The constraint is that there are two release vehicles with nothing in common\nbut this repository.** The server deploys in about a minute and is reversible\nin another. An iOS build goes through review, then through whenever each person\nchooses to update, and is not reversible at all. They can never ship together,\nwhich is the whole content of the two-step wire migration already written down:\nteach the server both shapes, deploy, ship the client, remove the old shape a\nrelease later.\n\n**And the App Store is not a version, it is a population.** This is the part a\nbranch cannot express and the reason the convention is mostly not branches. The\ndeploy history is full of sentences like \"build 30 kept working across the\nrestart\" and \"installed builds up to 35 call `GET /support` expecting JSON and\nnow receive HTML\" — the thing the server owes compatibility to is the oldest\nbuild still installed somewhere, which is neither the newest released build nor\nanything a ref points at.\n\n### What was chosen\n\n**`master` stays trunk, with short-lived branches merged back.** No develop\nbranch, no release branches. One person, a one-minute server deploy, and a\nrelease cadence measured in days: git-flow would be ceremony bought with\nnothing.\n\n**Builds are tagged, not branched.** A submitted build is immutable and is a\npoint in history — that is a tag. `build/36` was created retroactively on\n`b069d61`, the commit that set `buildNumber` to 36 and the `app/` that was\nactually archived; the three commits between it and the submission note touched\nonly `server/` and `planning/`, so `app/` and `core/` — the wire contract that\nbuild speaks — are unchanged across them. `bin/release-ios` now does it for\nevery upload.\n\n**`released` is the one branch, and it does not exist yet.** It points at what\nsomebody can download, so it is created when 36 is released rather than when it\nis approved — the two are separate decisions on purpose, which is why the\nrelease was set to manual. It earns its place for one reason that a tag does\nnot cover: if review rejects a build, the fix is made against what was\nsubmitted rather than against a trunk that has moved on.\n\n### The three gaps that were closed at the same time\n\nNone of this is worth much without knowing what is actually running, and it\nturned out nothing did.\n\n**`bin/deploy` rsyncs the working tree rather than a git ref**, deliberately —\n\"works from a dirty tree\" is in its own header comment, and it is the right\ncall for a one-command deploy. The cost was that the box held no record of what\nit was running: `/healthz` returned `{ok, audio}`, and every \"verified against\nproduction afterwards\" note in this file names a behaviour and no revision. It\nnow writes `server/deployed.json` before syncing, with the short sha, the\nbranch, and the time — the sha marked `-dirty` when the tree was, because the\ndeploys where that is true are exactly the ones somebody will later want to ask\nabout. It warns rather than refuses: refusing would take back the property the\nrsync was chosen for.\n\n**The deploy now checks that the box came back as the code that was sent.** A\nrestart that failed and left the previous process serving answers `/healthz`\nexactly like a successful one — and that is the failure most worth catching,\nbecause everything verified afterwards gets verified against the wrong\nrevision.\n\n**`bin/release-ios` bumped `buildNumber` and committed nothing**, so the commit\nthat became a build was identifiable only by reading `app.json` at each\nrevision. It now refuses a dirty tree, commits the bump before archiving so the\ncommit is what gets built rather than a description of it, and tags after the\nupload succeeds — the tag meaning \"Apple has this\", which nothing before the\nupload can promise. A failed archive therefore leaves a bump commit and no tag:\nthe build number is spent either way, since Apple will not take it twice, but\nno revision claims to be a build that does not exist.\n\n### The floor, and what it cannot do\n\n`MIN_SUPPORTED_BUILD` in `server/src/release.ts` is the oldest build the server\nstill answers correctly, and it exists to make one thing decidable: **a\ncompatibility shim may be deleted once the floor has passed the build that\nneeded it, and not before.** The two-step migration has always had a third step\nwith no rule attached, and this is the rule.\n\n**It is a declaration rather than a measurement, and that is a real limitation\nrather than a footnote.** Nothing on the wire carries a build number — the app\nsends no version header, the server records none — so the floor cannot be\nchecked against reality, and every claim that build N went on working was\nreasoned rather than observed. Making it measurable is in BACKLOG.md; it is a\nclient change, so it takes a build to reach anybody, and the builds already out\nthere will never send it. Absent will have to mean old.\n\nIt starts at 36 because nothing has ever been public: every earlier build is a\nTestFlight install on the author's own devices, updatable on demand, and\nTestFlight expires them at 90 days regardless. The moment 36 is released this\nnumber stops being free to move — after that, raising it means waiting for a\npopulation to turn over rather than deciding that it has.\n\nBoth it and the deployed sha are reported by `/healthz` and in the startup log.\nThey are unauthenticated, which was a choice: the question \"what is running on\nthe box\" is one worth being able to ask by curl from a machine that is not this\none, at the moment a deploy is being doubted, and a short sha of a private\nrepository is an opaque seven characters to anybody who does not already have\nthe repository.\n\n---\n\n"
}
```

# Backlog

Everything known and not done: work deliberately deferred, defects found and
left, behaviour nobody has tested. Every entry here is outstanding — if it has
shipped, it has moved to DECISIONS.md, and if it is about how to operate the
thing, it is in AGENTS.md.

Ordered roughly by size: the substantial pieces first, then individual defects.

The neighbours worth knowing about. **FEATURES.md** is the roadmap: features

file of its own, and that file is where it lives while it is being designed and
built, until it ships and whatever survives moves to DECISIONS.md.

considered and declined — several of which read like missing features until you

```
## Per-speaker volume

**Status:** not started. Noted 2026-08-10.

A way to adjust each speaker's volume individually — manually, or automatically
by levelling the decibels so every participant lands at a comparable loudness.
People's microphones, distances and voices differ, and today the only remedy is
the device volume, which moves everyone at once.

Two shapes, not mutually exclusive:

- **Manual.** A per-participant level control, applied at the listener. The
  playback track already has exactly this (`setVolume` scaling samples in
  passing), so the precedent exists; the question is where speaker audio can be
  scaled — client-side per subscribed track is the likely place, since the
  server forwards packets rather than decoding them.
- **Automatic.** Normalise so all speakers sit at a similar level. That means
  measuring loudness per track and applying gain continuously — real DSP,
  either on each client or by putting the server into the media path it
  currently stays out of.

Manual is the smaller step and would likely be client-only. Note the recording
is unaffected either way if the gain is applied at the listener: stems capture
what was published, not what any one listener chose to hear.

---

It cannot be checked: the app sends no version and the server\nrecords none, so the floor is a judgement about whose phone still has what, and\nevery claim in the deploy history that build N kept working across a restart\nwas reasoned rather than observed.\n\nThe shape is small.   "content": "import { mkdtempSync, rmSync, writeFileSync } from 'node:fs';\nimport { tmpdir } from 'node:os';\nimport { join } from 'node:path';\nimport { buildApp, type App } from '../src/app';\nimport { deployed, MIN_SUPPORTED_BUILD } from '../src/release';\n\n/**\n * What the box says it is. The point of all of this is that a running server\n * can be asked which revision it is, so the tests are about the answer being\n * present and honest rather than about any particular value.\n */\ndescribe('release identity', () => {\n  let app: App;\n\n  beforeEach(() => {\n    app = buildApp();\n  });\n\n  afterEach(async () => {\n    await app.fastify.close();\n  });\n\n  it('reports the compatibility floor and an unknown commit from a checkout', async () => {\n    const health = await app.fastify.inject({ method: 'GET', url: '/healthz' });\n    const body = health.json();\n\n    expect(body.ok).toBe(true);\n    expect(body.minBuild).toBe(MIN_SUPPORTED_BUILD);\n    // No deployed.json in a checkout, and that is not a failure: running from\n    // a working tree is its own answer to which revision this is.\n    expect(body.commit).toBe('unknown');\n  });\n\n  it('reads what bin/deploy stamped, and survives there being nothing to read', () => {\n    const dir = mkdtempSync(join(tmpdir(), 'thefloor-release-'));\n    const cwd = process.cwd();\n    try {\n      process.chdir(dir);\n      expect(deployed()).toBeNull();\n\n      writeFileSync(\n        join(dir, 'deployed.json'),\n        JSON.stringify({\n          commit: 'abc1234-dirty',\n          branch: 'master',\n          at: '2026-08-15T00:00:00Z',\n        })\n      );\n      expect(deployed()?.commit).toBe('abc1234-dirty');\n\n      // Garbage is the same as absent rather than a crash on boot: this is\n      // read on the startup path, and a server that will not start because a\n      // provenance file is malformed has traded a real thing for a label.\n      writeFileSync(join(dir, 'deployed.json'), 'not json');\n      expect(deployed()).toBeNull();\n    } finally {\n      process.chdir(cwd);\n      rmSync(dir, { recursive: true, force: true });\n    }\n  });\n\n  /**\n   * The floor is compared against iOS build numbers, which only ever rise and\n   * are integers. A float or a string would be a comparison that silently does\n   * the wrong thing at exactly one value.\n   */\n  it('keeps the floor an integer', () => {\n    expect(Number.isInteger(MIN_SUPPORTED_BUILD)).toBe(true);\n  });\n});\n"
}

<details><summary>result</summary>

```
## Keeping this file small, which is a standing job

**This file is loaded in full into every session, before anybody types
anything.** Nothing reads it in segments. Everything in `planning/` is read only
when somebody goes looking, so a paragraph there is free until it is needed and
a paragraph here is paid for every time. That asymmetry is the whole reason for
the split, and it decays quietly: the natural place to write down what just
happened is the file already open, which is this one.

**Keep it under 650 lines, and nearer 600.** It is 646 now: 728 earlier on
2026-08-15 when nine deploys' worth of narrative was moved out, then 617, and
back up with the branch conventions the same day. The headroom is about one
deploy wide on purpose: each new one displaces the last, so the file should sit
still rather than climb. **It is close to the limit now** — the next thing added
displaces something rather than joining it. When it passes 650, do not shave the traps —
almost all of the excess will be one of these:

- **Deploy narrative.** `## Deployment` keeps the *most recent* deploy and
  nothing else. When a new one lands, the one it replaces goes to
  `planning/DECISIONS.md` under `## The deploy history`, newest first, verbatim
  — the verification counts and the which-build-kept-working notes are the
  valuable part and are not to be summarised away in the move.
- **Reasoning about unshipped work.** Belongs in `planning/DECISIONS.md`, or in
  its own `planning/` design document if it is still being decided.
- **The story behind a rule.** Keep the rule and the cost of breaking it; move
  the account of the afternoon it cost, leaving a pointer.

What earns its place here is what stops somebody losing a day: `APNS_ENV`, the
three artifacts that disagree about entitlements, `rtc.use_external_ip`, where
each credential lives and what losing it costs, `prebuild --clean` dropping the
signing team. Those stay verbatim however long the file gets — the density of
the prose is not the problem, accumulation is.

Trimming is not a separate errand. Do it in the same commit as whatever added
the material, while the judgement about what is durable is still fresh.

**The `DECISIONS` volumes have a cap of their own: 2,000 lines each.** Not for
context — nothing loads them unprompted — but because a plain read stops at
2,000 and what it drops is the tail, which in an append-only file is the newest
material and the most likely to matter. The notice is easy to miss in a file
that reads like an archive. The single `DECISIONS.md` hit 2,433 lines on
2026-08-15 and was split; the live volume's header says how to close it and
start the next. Cut on a section boundary and on a seam that means something.

Line *length* is not a constraint worth thinking about — a read truncates at
2,000 characters and the prose here wraps at 79.

---
     648 AGENTS.md
```

</details>

```
# Working on The Floor

What you need before touching anything: how it is laid out, how to run it, how
to ship it, and the traps that have already cost somebody a day.

Everything that is not this file lives in **`planning/`**. This one stays at the
root because it is the one a fresh reader is pointed at; the rest are documents
you go looking for, and a root directory that lists them all buries the code.

Three of them answer a standing question each. **`planning/BACKLOG.md`** is what
is known and not done. **`planning/DECISIONS.md`** is what was built and why,
including what was deliberately not built. **`planning/FEATURES.md`** is the
roadmap: features that are wanted, at a paragraph each.

`DECISIONS` is **more than one file**. `planning/DECISIONS.md` is always the
live volume and the only one new decisions are appended to; closed volumes are
`planning/DECISIONS-<first date>-to-<last date>.md`, and the first is
`planning/DECISIONS-2026-08-07-to-2026-08-13.md`, which runs from the beginning
to the day the media server came off LiveKit Cloud. The live volume's header
carries the index and the rule for closing it. **Grep across the set** —
`planning/DECISIONS*.md` — rather than the live one alone, or you will search
only the last few days of the project's reasoning.

The rest are temporary, and say so in their own first lines. Designs for
unbuilt work — **`planning/ANONWEB.md`**, **`planning/WATCHPARTY.md`** — are
deleted when the work ships, with whatever survives moving to `DECISIONS.md`.
**`planning/APPREVIEW.md`** is the same kind of document for the first App Store
submission: what has to be built before it can be made, what is already settled
and where the reasoning is, and what has to be typed into App Store Connect. It
goes when the app is approved.

**`planning/DEMO-ACCOUNT.md`** is temporary in the same way, and narrower: the
two accounts App Review signs in as, why there are two rather than one, and the
order they have to be torn down in afterwards. Read it before deleting them, or
before touching `REVIEW_IDENTIFIER` / `REVIEW_CODE` on the box — unsetting those
before the accounts are gone is how the rows become unreachable. The credentials
are not in it; they are in `~/.config/thefloor/demo-account.txt`, mode 600, on
the same reasoning as the `.p8` keys.

Two are one-offs that stay. **`planning/POSTMORTEM-echo.md`** is the build 17
echo bug, start to finish. Read it before touching the iOS audio session —
three separate components configure it and the ways they disagree are not
guessable from the code. **`planning/MIGRATION.md`** is about moving this box:
it began as the 2026-08-13 migration to a *smaller* instance, built and then
abandoned before cutover when self-hosting the media inverted its premise, and
it now carries the sizing argument in both directions. Read it before sizing,
rebuilding or re-hosting the server, and before trusting `bin/provision`,
`bin/provision-livekit` or `bin/deploy`'s health check about any box that is not
the live one.

References between documents inside `planning/` are by bare filename, since
they are siblings. References from code and from this file carry the
`planning/` prefix.
```

</details>

Those stay verbatim however long\nthe file gets — the density of the prose is not the problem, accumulation is.\n\n**When the traps alone reach the limit, split thematically rather than shave.**\nTake a subject that a whole class of work never touches, move it to\n`planning/` entire, and leave a section here that names the traps it contains\nand says when to go read it. Nothing is summarised away, and the sessions that\ndo not need it stop paying for it — which is the same asymmetry the split from\n`planning/` was for, applied one level in. `planning/RELEASING.md` was the first\nof these and shows the shape: the seam is *who needs it*, not *how old it is*.\nA trap that bites outside the subject stays here even when it looks like it\nbelongs there — `APNS_ENV` is about push and reads like release material, and it\ncosts an afternoon to somebody testing locally who will never open the release\ndocument."
}

Committed and pushed. Sanity check: 739 tests green (server 384 → 387 with the new suite), typecheck clean across all three packages, both scripts pass `bash -n`, the deploy stamp round-trips through the server's parser, and `AGENTS.md` is 564 lines — back under its cap.

**Branch `worktree-release-branching`, commit `1b7ef36`.** Not merged — you're on `docs-split-decisions` and I'd rather you pick the target again.

#### The convention, as implemented

`master` is trunk. Builds are **tagged, not branched** — `build/36` now exists on `b069d61`, which is the `app/` that was actually archived; the three commits between it and your submission note touched only `server/` and `planning/`, leaving `app/` and `core/` untouched. `bin/release-ios` does it automatically from now on: refuses a dirty tree, commits the bump *before* archiving so the commit is what gets built, and tags only after the upload succeeds — so a failed archive leaves a bump commit and no tag, which is honest, since the build number is spent either way.

**`released` does not exist yet**, per the point you agreed with: build 36 is `WAITING_FOR_REVIEW`, so nothing is downloadable. Create it when you release.

#### What I found while implementing

Three gaps, and they matter more than the branch names. **Nothing knew what was running.** `bin/deploy` rsyncs the working tree by design ("works from a dirty tree" is in its own header), so the box held no record of its revision and `/healthz` returned `{ok, audio}`. It now stamps `server/deployed.json` with the short sha — marked `-dirty` when the tree was, because those are exactly the deploys someone later asks about — and deletes it locally after the sync, so a checkout never claims a deployed revision. `/healthz` and the startup log report it.

**The deploy now verifies the box came back as the code sent.** A restart that failed and left the old process serving answers `/healthz` identically to a successful one — the failure most worth catching, since everything you verify afterward gets verified against the wrong revision.

**`MIN_SUPPORTED_BUILD`** (`server/src/release.ts`) gives your two-step wire migration the third step it never had. Set to 36, with the reasoning inline — **that's the one value worth your review.** It's a declaration, not a measurement: no client sends a build number, so every "build 30 kept working" in your deploy history was reasoned rather than observed. Making it real is now in `BACKLOG.md`.

#### On your mid-turn note about `AGENTS.md`

It bit immediately — adding the branch section put the file at exactly 650, and its own rule says trim in the same commit. So I encoded your rule and executed it: **split by *who needs it*, not by how old it is.** `planning/RELEASING.md` takes 115 lines verbatim (the `app.json` settings, the icon alpha-channel rejection, `prebuild --clean` dropping the signing team, the submission state). File is 564.

The test I wrote into the rule, because it's the part that's easy to get wrong: `APNS_ENV` *reads* like release material and stayed, because it costs an afternoon to someone testing push against a local build who'd never open a release document. The seam is whether work that never opens the other file can still be bitten by what moved. The pointer left behind names the traps rather than the topic — "see RELEASING.md for release things" is one nobody follows.

One consequence: `planning/APPREVIEW.md` referenced `AGENTS.md` for the `voip` decision, which moved; I repointed it.

result:
Branch/tag convention implemented and documented on `worktree-release-branching` (1b7ef36, pushed): build tagging in release-ios with build/36 created retroactively, deployed-sha stamping through /healthz with restart verification, MIN_SUPPORTED_BUILD as the shim-deletion rule, and AGENTS.md split thematically to planning/RELEASING.md at 564 lines — 739 tests and typecheck green.

> _system:_ Goal was a release branching convention now that build 36 is submitted; it's implemented and pushed on worktree-release-branching, with tests green. Next: tell me which branch to merge it into, since you're on docs-split-decisions. (disable recaps in /config)

Done, and I have a mistake to report along with the result.

**What went wrong.** To run tests inside the worktrees I symlinked `node_modules` in from your checkout. `.gitignore` had `node_modules/` **with a trailing slash**, which matches directories but not a *symlink* of that name — so my `git add -A` committed three symlinks into `1b7ef36`, and checking out `master` materialized them over your real `node_modules`, leaving links pointing at themselves. That's why `npm test` went silent.

**Repaired.** I amended the tip commit to drop the tracked symlinks, changed the ignore rule to bare `node_modules` with a comment explaining why the slash was wrong, and reinstalled all three packages from their intact lockfiles. Verified: real directories back (605/213/283 entries), full suite green — core 154, app 198, server 387 — typecheck clean, and no `node_modules` path tracked anywhere. I also reverted a one-line `core/package-lock.json` annotation npm added during the reinstall, since it wasn't yours and wasn't asked for.

**Consolidation is complete.** `master` is at `73dffb7` and carries everything: the docs volume split, the invite-identifier validation, and the branch/tag conventions. Both worktrees are gone, `docs-split-decisions` and `worktree-release-branching` are deleted (both fully merged), and `git branch --no-merged master` is empty — nothing local is unsaved. Tag `build/36` exists. The only other branch is `self-host-media`, older than this session and already contained in `master`; I left it alone.

Your `planning/FEATURES.md` is still uncommitted in both its staged and unstaged states — hashes verified identical to before I started, twice over, since the amend would otherwise have swallowed it.

**Two things need you, both because they involve pushing.**

```
git push origin master
git push origin build/36
```

`master` is 21 commits ahead of `origin/master` and the tag exists only here. After that, delete the stale remote branches:

```
git push origin --delete worktree-release-branching
git push origin --delete worktree-invite-identifier-validation
git push origin --delete worktree-rename-support-donations
```

`origin/worktree-release-branching` matters most — it still holds the **pre-amend** commit with the bad symlinks, so anyone checking it out gets the same damage I just repaired. It's the one branch worth deleting promptly rather than eventually.

result:
Everything consolidated on local master 73dffb7 (docs split, invite validation, branch/tag conventions + build/36 tag), all worktrees and merged branches removed; I also broke your node_modules via committed symlinks from a trailing-slash gitignore gap, fixed the rule, amended the commit, and reinstalled — 739 tests and typecheck green.

**→ Bash**

````json
{
  "command": "cat > /Users/rodvandur/.claude/jobs/34f700a7/tmp/to-md.js <<'EOF'\n// Renders a Claude Code JSONL session transcript as Markdown.\nconst fs = require('fs');\n\nconst [src, dest] = process.argv.slice(2);\nconst records = fs.readFileSync(src, 'utf8').trim().split('\\n')\n  .map((l) => { try { return JSON.parse(l); } catch { return null; } })\n  .filter(Boolean);\n\nconst esc = (s) => String(s).replace(/\\r/g, '');\nconst fence = (body, lang = '') => {\n  const text = esc(body);\n  // A body containing ``` needs a longer fence, or it closes early.\n  let ticks = '```';\n  while (text.includes(ticks)) ticks += '`';\n  return `${ticks}${lang}\\n${text}\\n${ticks}`;\n};\n\nconst textOf = (content) => {\n  if (typeof content === 'string') return content;\n  if (!Array.isArray(content)) return '';\n  return content.filter((b) => b.type === 'text').map((b) => b.text).join('\\n\\n');\n};\n\nconst out = [];\nlet turn = 0;\nlet firstTs = null, lastTs = null;\nconst stats = { prompts: 0, replies: 0, tools: 0, thinking: 0, results: 0 };\n\nfor (const r of records) {\n  if (r.timestamp) { firstTs ??= r.timestamp; lastTs = r.timestamp; }\n\n  if (r.type === 'user') {\n    const c = r.message?.content;\n    const results = Array.isArray(c) ? c.filter((b) => b.type === 'tool_result') : [];\n    if (results.length) {\n      for (const res of results) {\n        stats.results++;\n        const body = typeof res.content === 'string'\n          ? res.content\n          : Array.isArray(res.content)\n            ? res.content.map((b) => (b.type === 'text' ? b.text : `[${b.type}]`)).join('\\n')\n            : JSON.stringify(res.content ?? '', null, 2);\n        out.push(`<details><summary>result${res.is_error ? ' — error' : ''}</summary>\\n\\n${fence(body)}\\n\\n</details>\\n`);\n      }\n      continue;\n    }\n    const t = textOf(c).trim();\n    if (!t) continue;\n    stats.prompts++;\n    turn++;\n    out.push(`\\n---\\n\\n## ${turn}. Rodrigo\\n\\n${t}\\n`);\n    continue;\n  }\n\n  if (r.type === 'assistant') {\n    const c = r.message?.content;\n    if (!Array.isArray(c)) continue;\n    for (const b of c) {\n      if (b.type === 'thinking') {\n        stats.thinking++;\n        out.push(`<details><summary>thinking</summary>\\n\\n${esc(b.thinking).trim()}\\n\\n</details>\\n`);\n      } else if (b.type === 'text') {\n        const t = b.text.trim();\n        if (t) { stats.replies++; out.push(`${t}\\n`); }\n      } else if (b.type === 'tool_use') {\n        stats.tools++;\n        const input = JSON.stringify(b.input, null, 2);\n        out.push(`**→ ${b.name}**\\n\\n${fence(input, 'json')}\\n`);\n      }\n    }\n    continue;\n  }\n\n  if (r.type === 'system' && r.content) {\n    out.push(`> _system:_ ${esc(r.content).trim().split('\\n').join('\\n> ')}\\n`);\n  }\n}\n\nconst header = [\n  '# Session transcript',\n  '',\n  'Generated by an agent from the Claude Code JSONL session log. Verbatim: every',\n  'prompt, reply, tool call and tool result, in order. The model\\'s reasoning and',\n  'the tool results are in collapsed blocks so the conversation reads straight',\n  'through; expand them for the detail.',\n  '',\n  '| | |',\n  '| --- | --- |',\n  `| Session | \\`${records.find((r) => r.sessionId)?.sessionId ?? 'unknown'}\\` |`,\n  `| Source | \\`${src}\\` |`,\n  `| Began | ${firstTs ?? 'unknown'} |`,\n  `| Ended | ${lastTs ?? 'unknown'} |`,\n  `| Prompts | ${stats.prompts} |`,\n  `| Replies | ${stats.replies} |`,\n  `| Tool calls | ${stats.tools} |`,\n  `| Tool results | ${stats.results} |`,\n  `| Reasoning blocks | ${stats.thinking} |`,\n  '',\n].join('\\n');\n\nfs.writeFileSync(dest, header + out.join('\\n'));\nconsole.log(JSON.stringify(stats), '→', dest);\nEOF\necho written",

Answered from the code rather than from the roadmap note. Here's what I found.

**The column is right, and it can't be either of the alternatives.**

`pending_invites` (`server/src/db.ts:158`) exists precisely for the case where *no account exists*, so an account id is impossible by construction — that's the table's whole reason for being, and the comment above it says so: storing the request whether or not the recipient exists is what stops the interface leaking which addresses have accounts here. If the column were an account id, a request to a stranger would have nothing to store, and the difference between "produced a row" and "produced nothing" would be a membership oracle you could query one guess at a time.

And it already *is* the email. `identifier` is this codebase's name for the sign-in address throughout — `accounts.identifier`, `otp_codes.identifier`, the `/auth/request-code` body, the `/contacts/request` body. Renaming this one column to `email` would make it the only table that calls the same value something different, and the resolution join at `server/src/accounts.ts:402` matches `pending_invites.identifier` against `accounts.identifier` directly, so the two names have to stay recognizably the same thing. The abstraction also isn't idle: if a phone number ever becomes a sign-in address, `identifier` accommodates it and `email` would have to be migrated.

**One real gap, found while checking this.** `/auth/request-code` validates with `isEmailAddress` (`server/src/app.ts:286`); `/contacts/request` does not (`server/src/app.ts:392-409`). It passes the raw string to `requestContact`, which — finding no account — writes it to `pending_invites` verbatim. So `bob`, `  `, or a 10KB string all become permanent invite rows that can never resolve, because signing up requires an email and nothing else ever matches them. Nothing sweeps the table; the only deletions are on successful resolution and on account erase. The client doesn't guard it either — `HomeView.tsx:684` is a plain text input labelled "Search by email address".
```

And the false turns are `<system-reminder>`-only records, not you. Rewriting the converter.

# Session transcript

Generated from the Claude Code JSONL log for this session. Every prompt,
reply, tool call and tool result, in order and in full.

Three things to know about what is here:

- **The model's reasoning is not in it.** Every `thinking` block in the log is
  an empty string carrying only a signature, so it is not persisted and cannot
  be recovered. What is below is what was said and done, not what was weighed.
- **Tool results and injected context are collapsed.** Expand them for the
  detail; the conversation reads straight through without.

Saved to `planning/TRANSCRIPT-2026-08-17.md` — 283 KB, all 13 of your prompts, 102 replies, 206 tool calls and 205 results (11 of them errors), in order.

Verified rather than assumed: zero stray headings once fence state is tracked, so the turn outline is navigable; no secrets — no key blocks, no credential-shaped strings, and the only sensitive-adjacent matches are 9 mentions of your host and static IP, both already in `AGENTS.md`.

Three things the conversion turned up that are worth knowing:

**The model's reasoning is not recoverable.** All 97 `thinking` blocks in the log are empty strings carrying only a signature. Claude Code doesn't persist the content, so no renderer can produce it — I dropped the blocks rather than emit 97 empty boxes, and said so in the file's header.

**Some apparent "user turns" aren't you.** Six records are `<system-reminder>` injections. A naive conversion attributes them to you as prompts — my first pass did, giving 19 turns instead of 13. They're preserved as collapsed "injected context" rather than discarded.

**One deliberate alteration**, documented at the top: heading levels inside replies are demoted by two, or they'd collide with the turn headings and wreck the outline. Nothing else is changed.

**On there being no conventional way** — I checked rather than trusting memory. `claude --help` on 2.1.226 has no `export` subcommand and nothing transcript-related; the JSONL under `~/.claude/projects/` is a *resume* format, optimized for replaying a session into a model, not for reading. Slash commands aren't enumerable from the CLI, so an in-session `/export` may well exist and is worth typing before reaching for my script. But the empty `thinking` blocks suggest why a first-class renderer is awkward: the on-disk record is deliberately not a complete account of the session.

Two loose ends, both yours to call. The file is **untracked** — I left it that way rather than commit a 283 KB transcript into `planning/` on your behalf. And the converter currently lives in this job's scratch directory, which disappears with the job; if you want this repeatable, say the word and I'll put it in `bin/` as something like `bin/transcript`.
